**Project context:** `{}`

**Question:** How does the Zoning Resolution instruct a reader to resolve a conflict between the enacted text and an illustration or summary table?

### Selected evidence

**ZR 12-01 — canonical section 20018521**

> The following rules of construction apply to the text of this Resolution:

(a) The particular shall control the general.

(b) In case of any difference of meaning or implication between the text of this Resolution and any caption, illustration, summary table or illustrative table, the text shall control.

(c) The word "shall" is always mandatory and not discretionary. The word "may" is permissive.

(d) Words used in the present tense shall include the future; and words used in the singular number shall include the plural, and the plural the singular, unless the context clearly indicates the contrary.

(e) A "building" or "structure" includes any part thereof. The terms residential building, commercial building and community facility building shall refer to an entire building used exclusively for such use.

(f) The phrase "used for" includes "arranged for", "designed for", "intended for", "maintained for", "or occupied for".

(g) The word "person" includes an individual, a corporation, a partnership, an incorporated association or any other similar entity.

(h) Unless the context clearly indicates the contrary, where a regulation involves two or more items, conditions, provisions, or events connected by the conjunction "and", "or" or "either...or", the conjunction shall be interpreted as follows:

(1) "and" indicates that all the connected items, conditions, provisions or events shall apply;

(2) "or" indicates that the connected items, conditions, provisions or events may apply singly or in any combination; and

(3) "either...or" indicates that the connected items, conditions, provisions or events shall apply singly but not in combination.

(i) The word "includes" shall not limit a term to the specified examples, but is intended to extend its meaning to all other instances or circumstances of like kind or character.

(j) References within a Section or cross-references to a Section numbered with four digits shall include all following Sections with numbers whose first four digits are identical with such Section number but references or cross-references to a Section numbered with five digits shall refer only to such specific five-digit Section. For Sections starting with 101-00, references within a Section or cross-references to a Section numbered with five digits shall include all following Sections with numbers whose first five digits are identical with such Section number but references or cross-references to a Section numbered with six digits shall refer only to such specific six-digit Section.

### Permitext answer

Across the selected Use Group I table, M1 is the broadest district for the listed open uses; M2 and M3 allow only agriculture and outdoor skating rinks as-of-right, while sand, gravel, or clay pits require a special permit in all three districts.

- **Agricultural uses** (including greenhouses, nurseries, and truck gardens): permitted in M1, M2, and M3, with both **P** (additional conditions) and **U** (open-use allowances).
- **Cemeteries, golf courses, and public or private parks/playgrounds:** permitted only in M1; not permitted in M2 or M3.
- **Outdoor racket courts:** permitted in M1 with **P**; not permitted in M2 or M3.
- **Outdoor skating rinks:** permitted in M1, M2, and M3 without a second-level table notation.
- **Sand, gravel, or clay pits:** marked **○** in M1, M2, and M3, meaning a special permit is required rather than as-of-right permission.

The first-level symbols mean: **●** permitted as-of-right; **♦** permitted with limitations; **○** special permit required; and **–** not permitted. A second-level notation identifies an added allowance or restriction on an as-of-right use: **S** for size restrictions, **P** for additional conditions, and **U** for open-use allowances. Under the selected table’s introductory text, an Open Uses entry that is permitted as-of-right must be unenclosed, except for ancillary buildings or other structures. The table does not use **♦** or **S** in these listed M1–M3 cells, but their meanings are provided in its legend. The selected table also directs that the notations are further described in ZR 42-10; that provision explains that a cell with a permitted symbol and no second-level notation is permitted without size restrictions, additional conditions, or open-use allowances, subject to the stated generally applicable provisions and possible supplementary regulations.

This is a summary of the selected table only; it does not determine the mapped district for any particular site or resolve the additional conditions or open-use regulations signaled by P or U.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- M1 permits more listed Use Group I open uses than M2 or M3: Agricultural uses are permitted with P and U in all three districts, and outdoor skating rinks are permitted in all three. Cemeteries, golf courses, outdoor racket courts (with P), and public parks or playgrounds or private parks are permitted only in M1 and are not permitted in M2 or M3. Sand, gravel, or clay pits require a special permit in each of M1, M2, and M3.
- Symbols identify the approval path and added restrictions or allowances: The table legend defines ● as permitted, ♦ as permitted with limitations, ○ as special permit required, and – as not permitted; S denotes a size restriction, P additional conditions, and U open-use allowances. ZR 42-10 further explains that the first-level symbol gives the use allowance and a second-level notation, where present, identifies restrictions or allowances on the as-of-right use. A permitted use with no second-level notation is permitted without size restrictions, additional conditions, or open-use allowances, subject to the generally applicable and supplementary provisions identified there.
- As-of-right Open Uses must be unenclosed: For uses listed under Open Uses, where the table permits the use as-of-right in a Manufacturing District, it must be unenclosed except for ancillary buildings or other structures.

**Assumptions**

- None

**Missing facts**

- None

**Citations**

- ZR 42-111: Selected governing table establishing the Use Group I allowances in M1, M2, and M3, the table legend, and the unenclosed requirement for as-of-right Open Uses.
- ZR 42-10: Supplied enacted cross-reference explaining the two levels of table notation and the consequence of a permitted cell without second-level symbology.

### Permitext answer

Appendix J establishes a map-based framework for certain designated areas within Manufacturing Districts. For self-service storage facilities, areas shown on Subarea 1 maps are subject to the as-of-right provisions of Section 42-19 (Use Group IX – Storage); areas shown on Subarea 2 maps are subject to a City Planning Commission special permit under Section 74-192. The tables identify each designated area by borough, community district, and applicable Appendix map number(s).

Subarea 1 identifies Bathgate, Steinway, Richmond Hill, West Shore, and Rossville. Subarea 2 identifies Port Morris, Hunts Point, Zerega, Eastchester, Brooklyn Navy Yard, Southwest Brooklyn, Flatlands/Fairfield, East New York, Ridgewood, Williamsburg/Greenpoint, North Brooklyn/Long Island City/Maspeth, Maspeth/North Brooklyn, Long Island City, Woodside, Jamaica, JFK, and North Shore.

No site-specific conclusion can be made from the selected material as to whether a property is within an Appendix J designated-area boundary, which map applies, or whether the Subarea 1 as-of-right path or the Subarea 2 special-permit path governs. That conclusion remains conditional on identifying the property by address or BBL and comparing its location and boundary to the applicable controlling official Appendix J map or other official mapped-location evidence.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Appendix J establishes the map-based storage framework: Appendix J states that boundaries of certain designated areas within Manufacturing Districts are shown on its maps. It places self-service storage facilities in two mapped categories: Subarea 1 areas are subject to the as-of-right provisions of Section 42-19 (Use Group IX – Storage), while Subarea 2 areas are subject to a City Planning Commission special permit pursuant to Section 74-192.
- Subarea 1 table identifies designated areas and map numbers: The Subarea 1 table identifies Bathgate in Bronx Community Districts 3, 4, and 6 on Map 1; Steinway in Queens Community District 1 on Maps 1 and 2; Richmond Hill in Queens Community District 9 on Map 1; West Shore in Staten Island Community Districts 1 and 2 on Maps 1 through 3; and Rossville in Staten Island Community District 3 on Map 1.
- Subarea 2 table identifies designated areas and map numbers: The Subarea 2 table identifies Port Morris, Hunts Point, Zerega, Eastchester, Brooklyn Navy Yard, Southwest Brooklyn, Flatlands/Fairfield, East New York, Ridgewood, Williamsburg/Greenpoint, North Brooklyn/Long Island City/Maspeth, Maspeth/North Brooklyn, Long Island City, Woodside, Jamaica, JFK, and North Shore by borough, community district, and Appendix map number or numbers.

**Assumptions**

- None

- The applicable controlling official Appendix J map or other official mapped-location evidence showing the property's position relative to the designated-area boundary and, if within a boundary, the applicable Subarea.

**Citations**

- ZR APPENDIX J: Establishes the Subarea 1 table entries, including borough, community district, designated-area name, and map number.
- ZR APPENDIX J: Establishes the Subarea 2 table entries, including borough, community district, designated-area name, and map number.

### Permitext answer

Within the Atlantic Avenue Subdistrict, ZR 101-75 bars the Department of Buildings from issuing a demolition permit for development unless both of these prerequisites are met:

- The owner has obtained building-application approval for the proposed new development or enlargement.
- The owner has presented evidence of a construction-financing commitment from a domestic bank, insurance company, or real estate investment company, in the form of a letter trust.

The exception is for buildings that are unsafe and whose demolition is required under Chapter 26, Title C, Part I, Article 8 (or its successor) of the New York City Administrative Code. The supplied evidence does not establish whether a particular building satisfies that unsafe-building exception.

ZR 101-04 establishes that the Atlantic Avenue Subdistrict is subject to the special regulations in ZR 101-70 through 101-75, in addition to other applicable Special Downtown Brooklyn and underlying-district regulations unless the subdistrict regulations specify otherwise.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Atlantic Avenue Subdistrict scope: The Atlantic Avenue Subdistrict is subject to the special regulations in Sections 101-70 through 101-75 and Appendices A through D, as well as other Special Downtown Brooklyn District and underlying-district regulations except where the subdistrict regulations provide otherwise.
- Demolition-permit prerequisites: For development within the Subdistrict, no Department of Buildings demolition permit may be issued until the owner has obtained building-application approval for the new development or enlargement and has presented evidence, in the form of a letter trust, of a construction-financing commitment from a domestic bank, insurance company, or real estate investment company.
- Unsafe-building exception: The demolition-permit restriction does not apply in the case of buildings that are unsafe and whose demolition is required pursuant to Chapter 26, Title C, Part I, Article 8, or its successor, of the New York City Administrative Code.

**Assumptions**

- None

**Missing facts**

- None

**Citations**

- ZR 101-04: Establishes that ZR 101-75 is among the special regulations applicable to the Atlantic Avenue Subdistrict.
- ZR 101-75: Establishes the two demolition-permit prerequisites and the unsafe-building exception.

### Expected conclusion

Section 101-04 establishes that Sections 101-70 through 101-75 apply to the Atlantic Avenue Subdistrict within the Special Downtown Brooklyn District. Section 101-75 requires building-application approval for the new development or enlargement. Section 101-75 also requires evidence of a construction-financing commitment from a domestic bank, insurance company, or real estate investment company, in the source's stated form of a letter trust. The exception is limited to unsafe buildings whose demolition is required under the cited Administrative Code provision or its successor.

### Permitext answer

NYC Planning’s official amendment-history metadata for ZR 42-00 identifies 12 events: the section was added effective December 15, 1961 (CP15820), and amended effective August 15, 1974 (CP22683); September 19, 1985 (N850720ZRY); October 25, 1993 (N930327CZRY); October 25, 1995 (N950384ZRY); September 9, 2004 (N040202ZRY); February 2, 2011 (N110090AZRY); October 10, 2013 (N130331AZRY); December 6, 2023 (N230113ZRY); twice on June 6, 2024 (N240010ZRY and N240011ZRY); and December 5, 2024 (N240290ZRY). The listed notes describe, among other things, removal of specified Use Groups in 2023; reorganization and relocation of provisions to Sections 42-10 through 42-20 and addition of Manufacturing Districts with an A suffix in 2024; and removal of a no-longer-applicable section and cross-reference updates in December 2024.

That is current official amendment-history metadata accompanying the current text, not a complete set of historical versions. Before reconstructing the text in force on a particular date, a professional must obtain and compare the actual adopted amendment text for each relevant CPC action and its effective-date/transition provisions, identify the immediately preceding operative version, and confirm whether an amendment changed, relocated, repealed, or merely cross-referenced the language at issue. The current text also expressly says that Chapter 2 use provisions may be modified or superseded by special rules in Article I, Article VI, and Special Purpose Districts; those potentially applicable rules must be checked separately for any project-specific conclusion.

The supplied current code edition is continuously amended and runs through August 13, 2026. It supports describing the current section, but it does not itself establish what Section 42-00 said on an earlier date.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Official amendment-history entries for Section 42-00: The official NYC Planning metadata lists one addition and 11 amendments for ZR 42-00, with effective dates, CPC report numbers, project labels or unavailable labels, notes, and report URLs. The two June 6, 2024 entries are distinct actions: N240010ZRY reorganized general provisions and relocated provisions to Sections 42-10 through 42-20, inclusive; N240011ZRY added applicability for Manufacturing Districts with an A suffix in paragraph (b)(2)(vi).
- Metadata does not establish historic operative text: The official metadata expressly states that it identifies amendment events and report links, accompanies the current section text, and does not reproduce every historical version of the section. It therefore cannot by itself establish the exact text in force on a selected historical date.
- Current Section 42-00 includes broader modification and supersession language: The current text states that Chapter 2 use provisions may be modified or superseded by special rules for certain areas in Article I, Article VI, and through Special Purpose Districts. This is a current-rule qualification; the supplied evidence does not establish the corresponding historical text or applicability of any such special rule on a particular date.

**Assumptions**

- None

- None

**Citations**

- ZR 42-00: Current Section 42-00 text, including its statement that Chapter 2 use provisions may be modified or superseded by certain special rules.

Special provisions for self-storage facilities In designated areas within Manufacturing Districts, as shown on the maps in APPENDIX J (Designated Areas Within Manufacturing Districts) of this Resolution, a self-service storage facility is subject to the provisions of this Section. Designated areas in which self-service storage facilities are subject to the as-of-right provisions of this paragraph are shown on the maps in Subarea 1, and those in which such uses are subject to special permit of the City Planning Commission pursuant to Section 74-192 (Self-service storage facility in designated areas within Manufacturing Districts) are shown on the maps in Subarea 2. A self-service storage facility shall, in Subarea 1 of APPENDIX J of this Resolution, be limited to establishments that provide an industrial floor space as defined in Section 12-10 (DEFINITIONS) or “business-sized” storage space as specified in paragraph (b)(2)(ii) of this Section. On a zoning lot greater than or equal to 50,000 square feet in area, a self-service storage facility shall provide industrial floor space that is: equal in floor area or cellar space to 25 percent of the lot area; located below the level of the third story, with at least 50 percent of such industrial floor space located on the ground floor, with such ground floor story located within five feet of curb level, or base plane, as applicable, and the remaining industrial floor space located on a level that is immediately above or below such story; and provided with access to freight elevators and the accessory off-street loading berth required for such industrial floor space in accordance with the provisions of Section 44-566 (Regulations for permitted or required loading berths for zoning lots containing self-service storage facilities in designated areas). On a zoning lot that on December 19, 2017, is less than 50,000 square feet in area, a self-service storage facility shall provide: industrial floor space as specified in paragraph (b)(1) of this Section; or floor area or cellar space containing securely subdivided space for lease within such self-service storage facility, where each subdivided space is not less than 100 square feet in area, and with a minimum clear height of eight feet. Such spaces shall be categorized as “business-sized” for the purposes of this Section and the number and sizes of such spaces shall be shown on plans filed with the Department of Buildings. The total area of such business-sized storage space shall be equal in floor area or cellar space to 25 percent of the lot area. On a zoning lot on which industrial floor space is provided in accordance with paragraph (b)(1) or (b)(2)(i) of this Section, an information sign shall be provided. Such required sign shall be mounted on an exterior building wall adjacent to and no more than five feet from all primary entrances of the building containing the industrial floor space. The sign shall be placed so that it is directly visible, without any obstruction, to persons entering the building, and at a height no less than four feet and no more than 5 feet 6 inches above the adjoining grade. Such sign shall be legible, no less than 12 inches by 12 inches in size and shall be fully opaque, non-reflective and constructed of permanent, highly durable materials. The information sign shall contain the name and address of the building in lettering no less than three-quarters of an inch in height, and the following statement in lettering no less than one-half inch in height: “This building is subject to Industrial Floor Space regulations which require a minimum amount of space to be provided for specific industrial uses.” The information sign shall include an Internet URL, or other widely accessible means of electronically transmitting and displaying information to the public, where the information required in paragraph (b)(4) of this Section is available to the public. On a zoning lot on which industrial floor space is provided in accordance with paragraph (b)(1) or (b)(2)(i) of this Section, no later than June 30 of each year, beginning in the first calendar year in which a temporary or final certificate of occupancy was issued for the industrial floor space, the owner of the building subject to the use restrictions of this Section shall prepare a report on the existing conditions of the building. Such report shall be in a form provided by the Director of the Department of City Planning, and shall provide the following information at the designated Internet URL, or other widely accessible means of electronically transmitting and displaying information to the public: the total floor area of the industrial floor space in the building required by this Section; the name of each business establishment occupying floor area reserved for the industrial floor space. Such business establishment name shall include that name by which the establishment does business and is known to the public. > On zoning lots in designated areas within Manufacturing Districts in Subarea 2, as shown on the maps in Appendix J (Designated Areas Within Manufacturing Districts) of this Resolution, the City Planning Commission may permit the development, enlargement not permitted pursuant to the provisions of Section 42-193 (Use Group IX – uses subject to additional conditions), or change of use of a building for self-service storage facility use listed under Use Group IX(A).

To grant such permit, the Commission shall find that the zoning lot is appropriate for such self-service storage facility use, based on the land use characteristics of the proposed zoning lot and the surrounding area. In making this determination, the Commission may consider the following:

whether such use is consistent with the economic development objectives of the City for the designated area in which the self-service storage facility seeks to be located, and may, in making this determination, consult with the Department of Small Business Services; whether recent trends for and levels of investment in “qualifying uses”, as defined below, demonstrate that there is minimal demand for space for such uses in the surrounding area; whether the size and configuration of the zoning lot make it better suited for self-service storage facility use than for “qualifying uses”; for changes of use to existing buildings, whether the design and layout of loading docks, interior column spacing, floor-to-ceiling height and other relevant physical characteristics of the existing building make the building better suited for self-service storage facility use than for “qualifying uses”; whether the distance of the zoning lot from an arterial highway or a designated truck route, or lack of frontage on a wide street, makes the zoning lot better suited for self-service storage facility use than for “qualifying uses”; whether the distance of the zoning lot from mass transit that serves employees makes the zoning lot better suited for self-service storage facility use than for “qualifying uses”; whether the establishment of a self-service storage facility will cause environmental remediation work to be undertaken on the zoning lot; or whether there is a concentration of existing self-service storage facilities in the surrounding area.

For the purposes of this Section, “qualifying uses” shall include industrial drycleaning and laundry services listed in Use Group VI, as well as uses listed in Use Group IX (other than a self-service storage facility), or Use Group X.

The Commission may impose appropriate conditions and safeguards to minimize any adverse effects upon the existing uses in the surrounding area.

### Permitext answer

No. A parcel-specific as-of-right finding cannot be made from the supplied facts. The self-service-storage row applies only in M1, M2, and M3 Districts and is marked “♦P, meaning it is permitted as-of-right only in some instances, subject to broad district or geographic limitations and additional conditions. Without a property identifier and controlling mapped-zoning evidence, the property cannot be placed in a qualifying manufacturing district.

Appendix J status is independently decisive. In designated Manufacturing District areas, Subarea 1 is the as-of-right route subject to ZR 42-192’s conditions; Subarea 2 instead requires a City Planning Commission special permit under ZR 74-192. The supplied Appendix J text and tables identify designated-area names and map numbers, but do not locate an unidentified property within any boundary. No Appendix J map image or authoritative mapped-location evidence is supplied.

If the property is established to be in Subarea 1, lot area and the December 19, 2017 lot-area fact determine the applicable path. A lot of at least 50,000 square feet must provide industrial floor space equal to 25 percent of lot area, with the stated below-third-story, ground-floor, freight-elevator, and loading-berth conditions. For a lot that on December 19, 2017 was less than 50,000 square feet, the facility must provide either that industrial-floor-space option or qualifying business-sized storage space totaling 25 percent of lot area; each leased subdivided space must be at least 100 square feet with an eight-foot minimum clear height. The December 19, 2017 language is a factual trigger in the current supplied provision; it is not evidence of historical substantive zoning law.

The unresolved special-district status also prevents a complete determination: the supplied passages do not establish the effect of any particular special-district regulation. Further, the P notation requires self-service storage to conform to the performance standards in ZR 42-40 through 42-48, whose text and project-specific compliance inputs were not supplied. Thus, even if mapping ultimately confirms a Subarea 1 location, this evidence does not establish complete as-of-right compliance.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- The manufacturing-district allowance is limited, not unconditional: The Use Group IX(A) row classifies a self-service storage facility as “♦P in each of the M1, M2, and M3 columns. The supplied use-allowance provision explains that “♦ means permitted as-of-right in some instances but subject to broad district or geographic limitations, while P denotes additional conditions. The row therefore cannot establish an unconditional as-of-right allowance for an unidentified property.
- Appendix J maps distinguish the as-of-right and special-permit routes: Within designated Manufacturing District areas, the Appendix J maps identify Subarea 1 areas where self-service storage facilities are subject to the as-of-right provisions and Subarea 2 areas where they are subject to a City Planning Commission special permit under ZR 74-192. The supplied Appendix J tables list designated areas, community districts, and map numbers, but they do not establish the mapped position of any specific unidentified parcel.
- Subarea 1 conditions depend on lot area and the December 19, 2017 fact: In Appendix J Subarea 1, a self-service storage facility is limited to an establishment providing industrial floor space or qualifying business-sized storage space. For a zoning lot at least 50,000 square feet, industrial floor space must equal 25 percent of lot area and meet the stated story-location, ground-floor, freight-elevator, and loading-berth conditions. For a zoning lot that on December 19, 2017 was less than 50,000 square feet, the facility must provide either the specified industrial floor space or business-sized storage space totaling 25 percent of lot area; each leased subdivided business-sized space must be at least 100 square feet with an eight-foot minimum clear height.
- Subarea 2 is a special-permit route: For a zoning lot in an Appendix J Subarea 2 designated area, the City Planning Commission may permit development, certain enlargement, or change of use for a Use Group IX(A) self-service storage facility under ZR 74-192. The Commission must make the stated appropriateness finding based on the zoning lot and surrounding-area land-use characteristics, and may impose conditions and safeguards. This is not an as-of-right route.
- The P notation carries performance-standard conditions: For uses denoted P in the Use Group IX table, other than the listed exclusions for boat storage and public parking garages or lots, ZR 42-193 requires conformance with ZR 42-40 through 42-48 performance standards. A self-service storage facility is denoted P and is not among those exclusions.

The proposal’s FAR is 4.0: 40,000 square feet of proposed floor area ÷ 10,000 square feet of site area = 4.0. That is the proposed ratio, not a determination that 4.0 FAR is permitted. The applicable maximum depends on the site’s mapped district and any applicable mapped designation, then on the enacted FAR provision for that district.

> floor area General Definition Last Amended 12/5/2024 “Floor area” is the sum of the gross areas of the several floors of a building or buildings, measured from the exterior faces of exterior walls or from the center lines of walls separating two buildings. In particular, floor area includes: basement space, except as specifically excluded in this definition; elevator shafts or stairwells at each floor, except as specifically excluded in this definition; floor space in penthouses; attic space (whether or not a floor has been laid) providing structural headroom of eight feet or more; floor space in gallerias, interior balconies, mezzanines or bridges; floor space in open or roofed bridges, breeze ways or porches, if more than 50 percent of the perimeter of such bridge, breeze way or porch is enclosed, and provided that a parapet not higher than 3 feet, 8 inches, or a railing not less than 50 percent open and not higher than 4 feet, 6 inches, shall not constitute an enclosure; any other floor space used for dwelling purposes, no matter where located within a building, when not specifically excluded; floor space in accessory buildings, except for floor space used for accessory mechanical equipment; floor space used for accessory off-street loading berths in excess of 200 percent of the amount required by the applicable district regulations; floor space that is not otherwise exempt pursuant to this Section and is, or is made, inaccessible within a building; floor space in exterior balconies or in open or roofed terraces if more than 67 percent of the perimeter of such balcony or terrace is enclosed and provided that parapets, railings or safety guards, whether applied singly, or in combination, shall not constitute an enclosure when meeting the following criteria: parapets shall not exceed four feet in height; railings shall not exceed 4 feet, 6 inches, and shall be at least 50 percent open for the portion that exceeds four feet in height; and safety guards shall not exceed 10 feet in height and shall be at least 90 percent transparent for the portion that exceeds four feet in height. In addition, where such balcony or terrace has a roofed portion above it, there shall be an opening that is not less than 40 percent of the height between the bottom of the roof and the finished floor level of such balcony. For the purposes of such calculation, exterior building walls on adjoining zoning lots abutting an open or roofed terrace shall not constitute an enclosure. A sun control device that is accessible for purposes other than for maintenance shall be considered a balcony; and any other floor space not specifically excluded. However, the floor area of a building shall not include: cellar space, except where such space is used for dwelling purposes. Cellar space used for retailing shall be included for the purpose of calculating requirements for accessory off-street parking spaces, accessory bicycle parking spaces and accessory off-street loading berths; elevator or stair bulkheads, accessory water tanks, or cooling towers, except that such exclusions shall not apply in R2A Districts; uncovered steps; attic space (whether or not a floor has been laid) providing structural headroom of less than eight feet; floor space in open or roofed bridges, breeze ways or porches, provided that not more than 50 percent of the perimeter of such bridge, breeze way or porch is enclosed, and provided that a parapet not higher than 3 feet, 8 inches, or a railing not less than 50 percent open and not higher than 4 feet, 6 inches, shall not constitute an enclosure; floor space used for accessory off-street parking spaces provided in any story: up to 300 square feet per single- or two-family residence, on zoning lots where individual parking spaces accessory to residential uses are provided; within group parking facilities located not more than 23 feet above curb level, except where such floor space used for accessory parking is contained within a public parking garage; or within automated parking facilities located not more than 40 feet above curb level, except where such floor space used for accessory parking is contained within a public parking garage; floor space used for accessory off-street loading berths, up to 200 percent of the amount required by the applicable district regulation; floor space used for accessory mechanical equipment. Such exclusion shall also include the minimum necessary floor space to provide for necessary maintenance and access to such equipment. For the purposes of calculating floor space used for mechanical equipment, building segments on a single zoning lot may be considered to be separate buildings; floor space in exterior balconies or in open or roofed terraces provided that not more than 67 percent of the perimeter of such balcony or terrace is enclosed and provided that parapets, railings or safety guards, whether applied singly or in combination, shall not constitute an enclosure when meeting the following criteria: parapets shall not exceed four feet in height; railings shall not exceed 4 feet, 6 inches, and shall be at least 50 percent open for the portion that exceeds four feet in height; and safety guards shall not exceed 10 feet in height and shall be at least 90 percent transparent for the portion that exceeds four feet in height. In addition, where such balcony or terrace has a roofed portion above it, there shall be an opening that is not less than 40 percent of the height between the bottom of the roof and the finished floor level of such balcony, For the purposes of such calculation, exterior building walls on adjoining zoning lots abutting an open or roofed terrace shall not constitute an enclosure. A sun control device that is accessible for purposes other than for maintenance shall be considered a balcony; floor space within stairwells: at each floor of buildings containing residences developed or enlarged after April 16, 2008, that are greater than 125 feet in height, provided that: such stairwells are located on a story containing residences; such stairwells are used as a required means of egress from such residences; such stairwells have a minimum width of 44 inches; such floor space excluded from floor area shall be limited to a maximum of eight inches of stair and landing width measured along the length of the stairwell enclosure at each floor; and where such stairwells serve non-residential uses on any floor, or are located within multi-level dwelling units, the entire floor space within such stairwells on such floors shall count as floor area; at each floor of buildings developed or enlarged after April 28, 2015, that are 420 feet or greater in height, provided that: such stairwells serve a space with an occupancy group other than Group R-2, as classified in the New York City Building Code, that is located at or above a height of 420 feet; and such floor space excluded from floor area shall be limited to: the 25 percent of stair and landing width required by the New York City Building Code which is provided in addition to the stair and landing widths required by such Code for means of egress; or the one stairwell required by the New York City Building Code which is provided in addition to the stairwells required by such Code for means of egress. For the purposes of this paragraph, such additional stairwell shall include the stair and landings as well as any walls enclosing the stair and landings; floor space used for the storage of equipment by the Fire Department pursuant to the New York City Fire Code, Section 511.7 (Storage space for pre-positioned department equipment) in buildings that are 420 feet or greater in height; qualifying exterior wall thickness; floor space in a qualifying rooftop greenhouse; floor space on a sun control device, where such space is inaccessible other than for maintenance; floor space within a fully electrified building or an ultra low energy building, of an amount equivalent to five percent of the floor area located within such building, and exclusive of any floor space otherwise excluded from floor area; floor space in buildings containing multiple dwelling residences allocated to building amenities, corridors, refuse storage or disposal, or access to elevated ground floor dwelling units that is provided in accordance with the provisions of Section 23-23, inclusive; floor space in Quality Housing buildings that was exempted pursuant the Quality Housing Program, as such program existed prior to December 5, 2024.
> floor area ratio General Definition Last Amended 2/2/2011 "Floor area ratio" is the total floor area on a zoning lot, divided by the lot area of that zoning lot. If two or more buildings are located on the same zoning lot, the floor area ratio is the sum of their floor areas divided by the lot area. (For example, a zoning lot of 10,000 square feet with a building containing 20,000 square feet of floor area has a floor area ratio of 2.0, and a zoning lot of 20,000 square feet with two buildings containing a total of 40,000 square feet of floor area also has a floor area ratio of 2.0)

**ZR 27-111 — canonical section 20022699**

> unds may be set forth in the guidelines.

Affordable housing regulatory agreement

An “affordable housing regulatory agreement” is a legally binding agreement between the owner of a building and a Federal, State, or local agency or instrumentality with respect to a development, enlargement, or conversion from non-residential to residential use, which:

requires all of the dwelling units, rooming units, or supportive housing units in such building to be income-restricted and occupied by an eligible household as established by such agreement for a period of no less than 30 years; for a rental building, restricts an amount of floor area that would otherwise be required for the MIH development, UAP development or qualifying residential site subject to affordability requirements for the life of such building, or, for a homeownership building, requires such building to be owned by a housing development fund corporation established pursuant to Article XI of the Private Housing Finance Law for the life of such building; and is entered into in connection with public funding.

HPD may impose additional requirements for buildings subject to an affordable housing regulatory agreement in the guidelines.

Affordable housing unit

An “affordable housing unit” is:

a dwelling unit, other than a super’s unit, that is used for class A occupancy as defined in the Multiple Dwelling Law, or a rooming unit, other than a super’s unit, that is used for either Class A or Class B occupancy as defined in the Multiple Dwelling Law, and that is or will be restricted, pursuant to an affordable housing regulatory agreement or restrictive declaration, to occupancy by: for a UAP site: households having an income less than or equal to a weighted average of 60 percent of the income index at initial occupancy: with no more than three income bands; no income band exceeding 100 percent of the income index; and for UAP sites with 10,000 square feet or more of affordable floor area, at least 20 percent of such affordable floor area at an income band of no more than 40 percent of the income index. However, with regard to preservation affordable housing, a grandfathered tenant shall also be permitted to occupy such affordable housing unit; or households as specified in an affordable housing regulatory agreement executed after December 5, 2024; or for an MIH site, qualifying households; a supportive housing unit within a supportive housing project.

Affordable housing units that are restricted to homeownership, as defined in Section 27-113, pursuant to an affordable housing regulatory agreement or a restrictive declaration, must be dwelling units.

Capital element

“Capital elements” are, with respect to any UAP site, the electrical, plumbing, heating and ventilation systems in such UAP site, any air conditioning system in such UAP site and all facades, parapets, roofs, windows, doors, elevators, concrete and masonry in such UAP site and any other portions of such UAP site specified in the guidelines.

Completion notice

A “completion notice” is a notice from HPD to the Department of Buildings stating that the affordable housing in all or a portion of any MIH site or UAP site is complete and stating the affordable floor area of such affordable housing.

Eligible common area

An eligible common area includes any residential floor area that is located within a super’s unit, and any residential floor area in such MIH site or UAP site that is not located within any other dwelling unit or rooming unit, but shall not include any residential floor area for which a user fee is charged to residents of affordable housing units.

Grandfathered tenant

A “grandfathered tenant” is any household that:

occupied an affordable housing unit in preservation affordable housing on the restrictive declaration date, pursuant to a lease, occupancy agreement or statutory tenancy under which one or more members of such household was a primary tenant of such affordable housing unit; and has not been certified to have an annual income below the income band applicable to such affordable housing unit; or in homeownership preservation affordable housing, has been certified to have an annual income below the income band applicable to such affordable housing unit, but has elected not to purchase such affordable housing unit.

In Mandatory Inclusionary Housing areas, grandfathered tenants may include tenants of buildings on an MIH site that have been or will be demolished, as set forth in the guidelines.
> claration, without regard to the date of any amendment.

Super’s unit

A “super’s unit” is, in any MIH site or UAP site, not more than one dwelling unit or rooming unit that is reserved for occupancy by the superintendent of such building.

UAP development

A “UAP development” (“Universal Affordability Preference development”) is a development or enlargement outside of a Mandatory Inclusionary Housing area that provides affordable housing or a supportive housing project that satisfies the requirements of this Chapter.

The residential floor area ratio in a UAP development may exceed that for standard residences set forth in Section 23-22 (Floor Area Regulations for R6 Through R12 Districts) only by the amount of affordable housing provided, either on the UAP zoning lot or, for UAP developments within a UAP Offsite Option Area, on a UAP site pursuant to paragraph (a) of Section 27-16 (Requirements for MIH Sites or UAP Sites).

However, UAP developments within a UAP Offsite Option Area may exceed the floor area ratios for standard residences set forth in Section 23-22 by utilizing affordable housing provided on a generating site, as such term was defined prior to December 5, 2024, at the rate set forth in Section 23-154, as such Section existed prior to December 5, 2024, provided that such generating site has vested pursuant to the provisions of Section 27-132.

UAP Offsite Option Area

A “UAP offsite option area” (“Universal Affordability Preference offsite option area”) is a former Inclusionary Housing Designated Area or R10 District outside of a former Inclusionary Housing Designated Area within which the limited UAP offsite option is applicable, pursuant to the regulations set forth for such areas in Section 27-00, inclusive. The locations of former Inclusionary Housing Designated Areas are identified in APPENDIX F of this Resolution.

UAP site

A “UAP site” (“Universal Affordability Preference site”) is a building that contains affordable housing or a supportive housing project for a UAP development

Any temporary or final certificate of occupancy issued after December 5, 2024, for a UAP site shall state that such building or portion thereof contains affordable housing, and shall state that such certificate of occupancy may be amended or superseded to reflect that the residential units in the building or portion thereof that are affordable housing units be used other than as affordable housing units only in accordance with the provisions of this Zoning Resolution.

UAP zoning lot

A “UAP zoning lot” (“Universal Affordability Preference zoning lot”) is a zoning lot that contains a UAP development and utilizes the floor area regulations of Section 23-22 (Floor Area Regulations for R6 Through R12 Districts) or the height and setback regulations of Section 23-432 (Height and setback requirements) applicable to qualifying affordable housing.

**ZR 27-16 — canonical section 20022711**

> Affordable housing in an MIH site or UAP site shall meet each of the requirements set forth in this Section for the entire regulatory period, except that affordable housing restricted pursuant to an affordable housing regulatory agreement shall only be required to comply with paragraphs (a) and (j) of this Section.

Location of MIH site or UAP site and MIH zoning lot or UAP zoning lot Where an MIH site or UAP site is not located within the MIH zoning lot or the UAP zoning lot, as applicable: the MIH site or UAP site and the MIH zoning lot or UAP development, as applicable, shall be located within the same Community District; or the MIH site or UAP site and the MIH zoning lot or UAP zoning lot, as applicable, shall be located in adjacent Community Districts and within one-half mile of each other, measured from the perimeter of each zoning lot. However, such bedroom mix requirements shall not apply to affordable independent residences for seniors. HPD may also waive such

### Permitext answer

Section 23-22 establishes two R7A residential FAR ceilings: 4.00 for standard residences and 5.01 for a zoning lot containing qualifying affordable housing or qualifying senior housing. On the stated 10,000-square-foot zoning lot:

- 10,000 sf × 4.00 = 40,000 sf.
- 10,000 sf × 5.01 = 50,100 sf.
- 48,000 sf ÷ 10,000 sf = 4.80 FAR.

The proposal is therefore 8,000 sf above the standard-residence ceiling and 2,100 sf below the higher table ceiling. It does not comply with the directly applicable standard-residence limit. It fits within the higher table ceiling only if the lot actually qualifies for that category; the statement that it contains qualifying affordable housing does not itself establish that result.

The qualifying route must be documented. Qualifying affordable housing includes only: an MIH development in a Mandatory Inclusionary Housing area, a UAP development, or a building subject to an affordable housing regulatory agreement. If relying on UAP, the development must be outside an MIH area and satisfy Chapter 27. Its FAR may exceed the 4.00 standard-residence FAR only by the amount of affordable housing provided. Thus, for the stated 48,000-sf proposal, the supplied UAP rule requires at least 8,000 sf of qualifying affordable housing to support the 8,000 sf above 4.00 FAR, in addition to the applicable UAP qualification and Chapter 27 requirements. The actual affordable-floor-area schedule and the legal basis by which that area qualifies remain necessary.

For a UAP site, an affordable housing unit must be the specified dwelling or rooming unit (other than a super’s unit) and be restricted by an affordable housing regulatory agreement or restrictive declaration. The supplied definition requires, at initial occupancy, a weighted-average income limit of 60 percent of the income index, no more than three income bands, and no band over 100 percent. If the actual UAP affordable floor area is 10,000 sf or more, at least 20 percent of that affordable floor area must be at an income band no higher than 40 percent of the income index. The 8,000-sf incremental-FAR calculation does not establish whether the total affordable floor area reaches that separate 10,000-sf threshold. The definition separately recognizes MIH-site qualifying households, supportive-housing units in a supportive-housing project, and requires affordable units restricted to homeownership to be dwelling units.

MIH and UAP-site affordable housing generally must meet every Section 27-16 requirement for the entire regulatory period. The supplied excerpt includes location limits, distribution across residential stories, common-primary-entrance requirements, bedroom-mix alternatives, stated exceptions and potential HPD waivers, and HPD authority to disapprove a configuration that segregates or stigmatizes affordable-housing residents. Those requirements do not alter the 8,000-sf arithmetic, but compliance is material to a UAP or MIH qualification conclusion. Affordable housing restricted under an affordable housing regulatory agreement has the stated narrower obligation to comply only with Section 27-16(a) and (j); paragraph (j) is not supplied.

If the asserted route is an affordable housing regulatory agreement, the executed agreement must establish the defined governmental counterparty, public-funding connection, income restrictions, and applicable 30-year or life-of-building commitments. If an offsite UAP arrangement is asserted, its location route also must be established: outside a UAP offsite option area, the UAP site must be within the UAP zoning lot. The supplied evidence does not establish that this lot is in an MIH area, a UAP offsite option area, or any other mapped area.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- R7A FAR ceilings and arithmetic: The Section 23-22 R7A row sets maximum residential FAR at 4.00 for standard residences and 5.01 for qualifying affordable housing or qualifying senior housing. For the stated 10,000-square-foot lot, those ceilings are 40,000 square feet and 50,100 square feet. The proposed 48,000 square feet is 4.80 FAR, 8,000 square feet above the standard ceiling and 2,100 square feet below the higher table ceiling.
- A qualifying category must be established: Qualifying affordable housing includes MIH developments in Mandatory Inclusionary Housing areas, UAP developments, and buildings subject to an affordable housing regulatory agreement. The listed routes, rather than a conclusory project description, establish the applicable qualifying category.
- UAP excess FAR is limited by affordable housing provided: A UAP development is outside a Mandatory Inclusionary Housing area and provides affordable housing or a supportive housing project satisfying Chapter 27. Its residential FAR may exceed the Section 23-22 standard-residence FAR only by the amount of affordable housing provided. Accordingly, the stated 8,000-square-foot increase over the 4.00-FAR baseline requires at least 8,000 square feet of affordable housing under this supplied rule, subject to the applicable UAP route and requirements. Outside a UAP offsite option area, the UAP site must be within the UAP zoning lot.
- Affordable-unit requirements can affect a UAP qualification: For a UAP site, the supplied affordable-housing-unit definition includes initial-occupancy income restrictions: a weighted average no greater than 60 percent of the income index, no more than three income bands, and no band exceeding 100 percent. Where the UAP site has 10,000 square feet or more of affordable floor area, at least 20 percent must be in an income band no greater than 40 percent. The passage separately identifies MIH-site qualifying households, supportive housing, and the rule that homeownership-restricted affordable units must be dwelling units. It also defines an affordable housing regulatory agreement and a completion notice that states completed affordable floor area.
- Section 27-16 obligations remain material for MIH and UAP routes: Affordable housing in an MIH or UAP site must meet each Section 27-16 requirement for the entire regulatory period, except that housing restricted under an affordable housing regulatory agreement need comply only with paragraphs (a) and (j). The supplied text includes location, unit-distribution, common-primary-entrance, bedroom-mix, waiver, and HPD-disapproval provisions. These rules do not change the FAR arithmetic, but their applicable compliance is material to establishing an MIH or UAP route. Paragraph (j) is not included in the supplied evidence.

### Expected conclusion

Section 23-22 sets an R7A maximum FAR of 5.01 for qualifying affordable housing or qualifying senior housing. A 5.01 FAR applied to 10,000 square feet permits 50,100 square feet, so the stated 48,000 square feet is below that table ceiling. Being below the table ceiling does not establish entitlement to the higher FAR; the applicable MIH, UAP, or regulatory-agreement path and its required amount of affordable floor area must be established under Article II, Chapter 7. For a UAP path, Section 27-111 limits the increase above the standard-residence FAR to the amount of affordable housing provided, so the project description alone cannot establish that the full proposed 4.80 FAR is available. The user's label alone is not treated as proof that the development qualifies for the higher FAR.

**Expected uncertainty:** conditional — The answer must preserve every qualification and evidence boundary stated in the reviewed concepts and avoid every forbidden conclusion.

### Permitext answer

No. Under the basic ZR 23-432 table, an R7A zoning lot containing standard residences has a 75-foot maximum base height and an 85-foot maximum building height. A 90-foot building exceeds that basic-table maximum by 5 feet.

A street-wall portion above 75 feet must be set back, with the setback provided at a height not lower than 40 feet and not higher than 75 feet. The table’s separate 115-foot maximum applies to qualifying affordable housing or qualifying senior housing, not standard residences.

ZR 23-434 does not create an eligible-site modification for R7A: its modification is for listed districts without a letter suffix, and its table lists R7-1 and R7-2, not R7A. Thus, based on the supplied basic height-and-setback provisions, 90 feet is not permitted for a standard-residence building in R7A.

For comparison only, the adjacent R7-1/R7-2 table categories are not R7A: their standard-residence maximums are 85 feet within 100 feet of a wide street and 75 feet on the specified narrow-street or beyond-100-foot portions. Neither allows 90 feet under the basic table.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- R7A standard-residence height limit: The R7A row sets a 40-foot minimum base height, a 75-foot maximum base height, and an 85-foot maximum height of buildings or other structures for standard residences. Therefore, a 90-foot building exceeds the applicable basic-table maximum by 5 feet.
- Setback for height above the base: For a building street-wall portion that exceeds the maximum base height, ZR 23-432 requires a setback at a height not lower than the minimum base height and not higher than the maximum base height. In the R7A standard-residence row, that range is from 40 to 75 feet.
- Qualifying-housing height column is separate: For the R7A row, the separate column for qualifying affordable housing or qualifying senior housing specifies an 85-foot maximum base height and a 115-foot maximum building height. Those values do not govern zoning lots containing standard residences.
- Eligible-site modification excludes R7A: ZR 23-434 permits its eligible-site height-and-setback modifications in the specified districts without a letter suffix, subject to its site criteria. Its table includes R7-1 and R7-2 but does not include R7A. It therefore supplies no eligible-site height allowance for R7A.

- None

**Missing facts**

- None

**Citations**

- ZR 23-432: Establishes the R7A standard-residence maximum building height, base-height values, the separate qualifying-housing column, and the setback trigger.
- ZR 23-434: Establishes that the eligible-site modification is limited to listed districts without a letter suffix and does not list R7A.

A 90-foot standard residential building exceeds the basic maximum by five feet. Section 23-434 is not used to enlarge the R7A limit because its eligible-site modifications apply only in listed districts without a letter suffix.

### Permitext answer

No—under the basic lot-coverage regulation, 8,500 square feet cannot be covered. For a standard interior lot in an R7 district, the maximum residential lot coverage is 80 percent. The calculation is 10,000 sq. ft. × 80% = 8,000 sq. ft. permitted coverage. The proposed 8,500 sq. ft. is 85% of the lot (8,500 ÷ 10,000 = 85%), which is 500 sq. ft. above the basic maximum.

This is the basic-rule result only. ZR 23-363 can increase the limit in defined circumstances. A shallow-lot increase requires eligibility for the shallow interior-lot rear-yard modification: the lot must be less than 95 feet deep at some point, the shallow condition must have existed on December 15, 1961, and it must not subsequently have increased or decreased in depth. The increase is one percent for every five feet below 95 feet, and the maximum for an interior lot ordinarily cannot exceed 90 percent. If a front or rear lot line meets a side lot line at an angle other than 90 degrees, depth must be measured at the midpoint of that irregularly angled lot line. Separate rules allow 100-percent coverage only in the specified portions within 100 feet of a qualifying corner or, where the front lot line coincides with the street line of the short dimension of the block, within 100 feet of that street line. None of those special conditions is established here, so they do not change the answer under the basic regulation.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Basic maximum for a standard interior lot: For standard lots in R6 through R12 districts, including R7, the maximum residential lot coverage for an interior lot or through lot is 80 percent. The corresponding maximum for a corner lot is 100 percent.
- Defined increases for certain interior or through lots: The otherwise applicable maximum may be increased for a zoning lot eligible for the specified shallow-lot rear-yard modification by one percent for every five feet of depth below 95 feet for an interior lot. The maximum coverage of an interior or through lot ordinarily may not exceed 90 percent. Where a front or rear lot line intersects a side lot line at an angle other than 90 degrees, depth is measured at the midpoint of that irregularly angled lot line. Separate 100-percent rules apply only to the described portions within 100 feet of qualifying corners or street lines along the short dimension of the block.
- Shallow interior-lot rear-yard eligibility: For an interior lot, the shallow-lot rear-yard modification applies where the lot is less than 95 feet deep at any point, the shallow condition existed on December 15, 1961, and it has not subsequently increased or decreased in depth. The required rear-yard depth may then be reduced by six inches per foot below 95 feet, but not below 10 feet.

**Assumptions**

- None

**Missing facts**

- Whether the interior lot qualifies for the shallow-lot rear-yard modification: its depth at the relevant point, the required historical existence of the shallow condition on December 15, 1961, and whether that condition has subsequently increased or decreased in depth.
- If a front or rear lot line intersects a side lot line at an angle other than 90 degrees, the lot depth measured at the midpoint of that irregularly angled lot line.
- Whether any proposed coverage lies within 100 feet of the intersection of two street lines meeting at an angle of 135 degrees or less.
- Whether the front lot line coincides with the street line of the short dimension of the block, and whether any proposed coverage lies within 100 feet of that street line.

**Citations**

- ZR 23-362: Establishes the 80-percent maximum residential lot coverage for standard interior lots in R6 through R12 districts.
- ZR 23-363: Establishes the shallow-lot coverage increase, midpoint depth-measurement rule for irregularly angled lot lines, 90-percent ceiling, and separate qualifying 100-percent portions.
- ZR 23-342: Establishes eligibility conditions for the shallow interior-lot rear-yard modification referenced by the coverage-increase provision.

- [x] concept-1: The answer accurately states that the standard interior-lot maximum in R6 through R12 districts, including R7, is 80 percent under ZR 23-362. (high confidence; objective).
- [x] concept-2: The answer correctly calculates 80 percent of 10,000 square feet as 8,000 square feet and correctly concludes that 8,500 square feet exceeds that basic maximum by 500 square feet. (high confidence; objective).
- [x] concept-3: The answer identifies all requested categories of ZR 23-363 modifications: shallow-lot increases, qualifying areas near corners, and short-block-dimension street-line areas. (high confidence; objective).
- [ ] concept-4: The answer does not state that rear-yard and other open-area requirements may be more restrictive than the 80-percent coverage calculation. Instead, it describes 8,000 square feet as 'permitted coverage'; its discussion of ZR 23-342 is limited to eligibility for a special coverage increase rather than explaining that yard requirements can constrain the footprint under the basic rule. Its ZR 23-342 discussion concerns only the special shallow-lot eligibility trigger. (high confidence; objective). ### Expected conclusion

Section 23-362 sets the basic maximum residential lot coverage for a standard interior or through lot in R6 through R12 Districts at 80 percent. Eighty percent of the stated 10,000-square-foot lot is 8,000 square feet, so 8,500 square feet exceeds the basic maximum. Section 23-363 can modify the percentage for specified shallow lots, locations near corners, and short block dimensions. The answer does not treat the 8,000-square-foot percentage calculation as an entitlement to that footprint because yard and other open-area rules may be more restrictive.

No definitive conclusion can be made from the stated facts. A 150-foot through lot does not meet the stated exception for a through lot under 110 feet in maximum street-to-street depth. If the shallow-lot condition is established, however, 20 feet is enough on the supplied depth rule: the lot is 40 feet short of 190 feet, so the rear yard equivalent may be reduced by 40 feet, but never below 20 feet. That reduces both the 40-foot requirement at or below 75 feet and the stated 60-foot requirement above 75 feet, where permitted, to 20 feet.

The historical condition is essential to that reduction. The lot must be less than 190 feet deep at some point, its shallow condition must have existed on December 15, 1961, and that condition must not subsequently have increased or decreased in depth. With that history unknown, the 20-foot proposal cannot be confirmed under the shallow-lot provision.

ZR 23-343 also makes the general rule subject to Section 23-34, inclusive, and separately says that rear-yard-equivalent regulations do not apply to large sites, certain full-block-frontage zoning lots with a through-lot portion contiguous to two corner-lot portions, or zoning lots occupying an entire block. None of those alternative exceptions is established here, so they cannot confirm the proposal—but the missing historical condition is not necessarily the only possible route to a compliant 20-foot area.

Even if a 20-foot depth is authorized, the supplied passage does not establish compliance without the applicable height category, a qualifying location/configuration, and an open area unobstructed from its lowest level to the sky except for the stated permitted-obstruction allowance.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- A shallow-lot reduction has historical prerequisites: For a through lot less than 190 feet deep at any point, the required rear yard equivalent may be reduced only where the shallow-lot condition existed on December 15, 1961, and subsequently neither increased nor decreased in depth. The reduction is one foot for each foot by which the zoning lot or portion is less than 190 feet, but the required yard or portion may not be reduced below 20 feet.
- A 150-foot depth reaches the 20-foot floor if the shallow-lot rule applies: The stated 150-foot depth is 40 feet below 190 feet. If the historical shallow-lot prerequisites are met, that permits a 40-foot reduction. The standard requirement is 40 feet for buildings or portions at or below 75 feet and, above 75 feet where permitted, 60 feet; applying the reduction reaches the express 20-foot minimum in either case.
- Other applicability paths and physical conditions remain material: The general through-lot rule is subject to Section 23-34, inclusive. The passage also exempts through lots under 110 feet in maximum depth, large sites, specified full-block-frontage corner-lot configurations, and zoning lots occupying an entire block. It requires the standard rear yard equivalent to be midway, or within 10 feet of midway, between street lines; eligible shallow lots may use the stated alternative street-line or side-lot-line open-area options. Any rear yard equivalent must be unobstructed from its lowest level to the sky except as provided for permitted obstructions.

**Assumptions**

- None

**Missing facts**

- Whether the shallow-lot condition has increased or decreased in depth since December 15, 1961.

- Whether the lot is a large site.
- Whether the zoning lot has a through-lot portion contiguous on one side to two corner-lot portions and occupies the entire block frontage of a street.
- Whether the zoning lot occupies an entire block.

- Whether the proposed open area is unobstructed from its lowest level to the sky apart from permitted obstructions.

### Permitext answer

No—on the stated facts, 30 feet is not sufficient under the general rule. For two buildings on one zoning lot that do not connect at any level, portions lower than 125 feet must be 40 feet apart, measured between their closest points. The stated 30-foot separation is therefore 10 feet short.

That conclusion assumes the space is not a qualifying rear yard equivalent, because buildings separated by a rear yard equivalent are excepted from this section. If the buildings are subject to the separate sky-exposure-plane-building rule, that rule modifies the spacing requirement based on wall/window condition and height, but its supplied table requires at least 40 feet in every listed condition; 30 feet would still be inadequate.

This is a general application of the supplied text, not a parcel-specific zoning determination. The address or BBL and controlling official zoning-map/district evidence are needed to confirm whether a mapped district or other applicable zoning provision changes the governing path. A site plan should also verify the stated 30 feet is the distance between the closest points of the two buildings.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Unconnected multifamily buildings below 125 feet require 40 feet of separation: For multiple buildings on one zoning lot that do not connect at any level, the minimum distance for portions lower than 125 feet is 40 feet, measured between the closest points. Because each stated building contains more than three dwelling units, the rule for buildings containing three or more dwelling units applies. A 30-foot closest-point distance is 10 feet below that minimum.
- Rear-yard-equivalent exception and sky-exposure-plane variation: ZR 23-371 does not apply to buildings separated by a rear yard equivalent. Separately, for sky exposure plane buildings, ZR 23-735 modifies the otherwise applicable unconnected-building spacing rule according to building height and facing wall condition; the supplied table has a minimum listed distance of 40 feet, so it does not make a 30-foot separation sufficient.

**Assumptions**

- None

**Missing facts**

- Whether the space between the buildings qualifies as a rear yard equivalent, which would place the buildings within ZR 23-371’s stated exception.
- The property address or BBL and controlling official zoning-map/district evidence needed to confirm the parcel’s mapped zoning applicability and whether another district-specific provision governs.

**Citations**

- ZR 23-371: Governing general minimum-distance rule for two or more buildings on one zoning lot that are not connected at any level, including the 40-foot requirement below 125 feet and the rear-yard-equivalent exception.
- ZR 23-735: Supplied enacted variation for sky exposure plane buildings; its table does not provide a spacing requirement below 40 feet.

**Question:** A zoning lot assembled in 2026 straddles two zoning districts, with more than 50 percent of its area in the less restrictive district. Can that majority district's use regulations automatically apply to the entire zoning lot under Section 77-11?

### Selected evidence

**ZR 77-02 — canonical section 20018891**

> Whenever a zoning lot is divided by a boundary between two or more districts and such zoning lot did not exist on December 15, 1961, or any applicable subsequent amendment thereto, each portion of such zoning lot shall be regulated by all the provisions applicable to the district in which such portion of the zoning lot is located. However, the provisions of Section 77-22 (Floor Area Ratio) shall apply to zoning lots created at any time where different bulk regulations apply to different portions of such zoning lot.

**ZR 77-11 — canonical section 20018894**

> Whenever a zoning lot existing on December 15, 1961, or on any applicable subsequent amendment thereto, is divided by a boundary between districts in which different uses are permitted, the use regulations applicable to the district in which more than 50 percent of the lot area of the zoning lot is located may apply to the entire zoning lot, provided that the greatest distance from the mapped district boundary to any lot line of such zoning lot in the district in which less than 50 percent of its area is located does not exceed 25 feet. Such distance shall be measured perpendicular to the mapped district boundary.

Whenever the use regulations are so applied, the district boundary may be assumed to be relocated accordingly, and the bulk, off-street parking and loading, and all other regulations applying to such expanded district shall apply to the entire zoning lot. However, when the zoning lot is divided by a district boundary between a district limited to single- or two-family residences and a district permitting multiple dwellings, the use and bulk regulations of an R3-2 District shall apply in the R1, R2, R3A, R3X or R3-1 portion, and the use and bulk regulations of an R4 District shall apply in the R2X, R4A, R4-1 or R4B portion.

Except as specifically provided by the provisions of a special purpose district, the provisions of this Section shall apply to zoning lots which are divided by a special purpose district boundary line.

**ZR 77-22 — canonical section 20018899**

> The maximum floor area ratio permitted on each portion of such zoning lot for the applicable type of building or buildings on such zoning lot shall be determined under the applicable regulations of this Resolution.

Each such floor area ratio shall be multiplied by the percentage of the zoning lot to which such floor area ratio applies. The sum of the products thus obtained shall be the adjusted maximum floor area ratio applicable to such zoning lot.

The floor area resulting from application of the adjusted maximum floor area ratio may be located anywhere on the zoning lot, subject to all other regulations of this Resolution, and provided that the floor area ratio for any portion of the zoning lot within one district shall not exceed the maximum floor area ratio, specified for that district, or the adjusted maximum floor area ratio for the zoning lot, whichever is greater, except that the portion of the zoning lot fronting on and within 100 feet of a wide street and permitting the greater maximum permitted residential floor area ratio may exceed the maximum permitted residential floor area ratio for the portion of the zoning lot by up to 20 percent.

In applying the provisions of this Section, the following conditions shall apply:

the floor area bonus permitted for publicly accessible open areas or arcades, under the applicable regulations of this Resolution, shall apply only to such publicly accessible open areas, arcades or portions thereof, as are located in a district in which such bonus is granted; and when a zoning lot contains a sky exposure plane building which does not have a specified maximum floor area ratio, for the purpose of computing the adjusted maximum floor area ratio, the floor area ratio of such zoning lot shall be deemed to be that which can be achieved at the minimum required open space ratio for such zoning lot.

### Permitext answer

No. Section 77-11 never makes the majority district’s use regulations automatic: it says they may apply only if its conditions are met. Those conditions include that the zoning lot existed on December 15, 1961, or on an applicable subsequent amendment date; that the boundary separates districts in which different uses are permitted; that more than 50 percent of lot area is in that district; and that the greatest perpendicular distance from the mapped boundary to any lot line in the minority portion is no more than 25 feet.

A 2026 assembly alone does not establish whether the zoning lot existed on every potentially applicable subsequent amendment date. If it did not exist on the applicable date, Section 77-02 requires each portion to be regulated by all provisions applicable to the district where that portion lies. If it did exist on the applicable date and all Section 77-11 conditions are met, Section 77-11 still uses discretionary language ( may apply”), not an automatic majority-district rule.

FAR is separately governed. For zoning lots created at any time with different bulk regulations on different portions, Section 77-02 directs use of Section 77-22: determine each portion’s applicable FAR, multiply it by that portion’s lot-area percentage, and add the products for the adjusted maximum FAR. That is not an extension of the majority district’s FAR across the entire lot.

The actual mapped district boundary, the minority-area depth measurement, the relevant amendment date, and any special-purpose-district provision must be verified from the property-specific official zoning-map and amendment record.

**What the selected evidence establishes**

- Section 77-11 is conditional and discretionary: Section 77-11 provides that majority-district use regulations may apply to the entire lot, rather than automatically applying. The rule is limited to a zoning lot existing on December 15, 1961, or on an applicable subsequent amendment date; divided between districts with different permitted uses; with more than 50 percent of its area in one district; and with a maximum perpendicular distance of 25 feet or less from the mapped district boundary to every lot line in the minority portion. If use regulations are so applied, the expanded district’s bulk, off-street parking and loading, and other regulations apply to the entire lot, subject to the stated single- or two-family/multiple-dwelling proviso. The section applies across a special-purpose-district boundary except as specifically provided by that district’s provisions.
- Portion-by-portion rule where the lot did not exist on the applicable date: Where a zoning lot divided by district boundaries did not exist on December 15, 1961, or on an applicable subsequent amendment date, each portion must be regulated by all provisions applicable to the district in which that portion is located. Assembly in 2026 does not by itself resolve whether the lot existed on every applicable subsequent amendment date.
- FAR uses an adjusted portion-based calculation: For a split zoning lot where different bulk regulations apply, Section 77-02 directs application of Section 77-22 regardless of when the zoning lot was created. Section 77-22 determines FAR for each portion, multiplies each FAR by the percentage of lot area to which it applies, and sums those products for the adjusted maximum FAR. Resulting floor area may be located anywhere on the lot, subject to the stated district-specific cap, other Resolution regulations, and the specified wide-street residential exception.

**Assumptions**

- None

**Missing facts**

- A usable property identifier, such as address or BBL, and controlling official zoning-map evidence showing the actual mapped district boundary and district designations affecting the zoning lot.
- Whether the zoning lot existed on the applicable subsequent amendment date for the district boundary at issue.
- The greatest perpendicular distance from the mapped district boundary to every lot line in the minority-area portion of the zoning lot.
- Whether the boundary is a special-purpose-district boundary and, if so, the applicable special-purpose-district provision that could specifically modify Section 77-11.

**Citations**

- ZR 77-02: Establishes the portion-by-portion regulatory rule for a split zoning lot that did not exist on December 15, 1961, or an applicable subsequent amendment date, and directs Section 77-22 for FAR.
- ZR 77-11: Establishes the conditional, discretionary majority-area rule for use regulations, its 25-foot measurement condition, and its stated provisos.
- ZR 77-22: Establishes the adjusted, portion-based FAR calculation and limitations.

### Expected conclusion

Section 77-11's majority-district use treatment applies only to qualifying zoning lots existing on December 15, 1961 or on the date of an applicable subsequent amendment. For the stated lot created in 2026, Section 77-02 generally regulates each portion under the district in which it is located. Section 77-22 separately applies an adjusted maximum FAR calculation to divided zoning lots created at any time. The answer distinguishes use-regulation treatment from the separate adjusted-FAR mechanism.

If such zoning lot, however, is occupied by a non-complying building, such zoning lot may be subdivided provided such subdivision does not create a new non-compliance or increase the degree of non-compliance of such building.
